Playing from a row starts its phrase where the order first plays the row, so a reverse phrase's top row is its last, and in a dealt order where the chain's first deal does, or at the phrase's start if `Random` doesn't pick it. **Unchanged:** a phrase without an order is forward, which walks the same visits as rows, so every song without one plays sample for sample as before and both golden renders are unchanged. **Alternatives:** a direction beside the order, as the Metropolix has, above; an order per track, as the Digitakt II's direction, which a track's chain of several phrases would make coarser than a phrase's; an order per chain slot, left for later; random orders from the track's generator, which would change how `CHA` rolls and couldn't be worked out from a step; a pendulum alternating direction each pass so its passes keep their length, which can't leave out its end rows' repeats; and `Random` making as many picks as the rows, whose pass length would vary with the rows' pulses. Added on 2026-11-09. **Replaced in part** by decision 91, the same day: an order is now a walk the track keeps taking, and a pass is the phrase's length in steps whatever the order, so what this says of a pass being one time round the order, of `Pendulum`, `Ping-pong` and `Pedal` lengthening passes, chains and sections, of `Random` cutting its last pick short, of conditions' passes, of playing from a row and of live changes gives way to it, and `Pendulum` and `Ping-pong` are now `Up-down` and `Up-down-inc`.
88. **A slot's bypass is a setting a step can lock, `1BYP` to `3BYP`, and switching a slot out or in is a straight 5 ms crossfade** (#266's first item, from #229's audit, Renoise's `x000` and `x001` done as a lock). **The setting:** it's in the registry as each slot's own, `Owner::Bypass`, rather than one of each effect's settings, as the slot keeps its bypass whatever effect is put in it, and no effect has to declare it. It's a list, `In` at `00` and `Bypassed` at `01`, stored as `1.bypass` and labelled with the slot's digit, as an effect's settings are; `BYP` was free, and no event's label is three letters that clash with it. So locks, holds, lanes, `K`, typing the label and the step text all come from the registry: a lock switches the slot for its note, the next note brings back what `b` set on the FX page, and held with `h` it stays until playing next starts from the top. It exists while its slot has an effect in it, so `K` offers it only then, and it's listed with that effect's settings, under `FX 1 Delay`, through a new `group_on` that knows the track. It's left out of LFOs' and envelopes' `Dest`, as whether a slot is in is chosen rather than swept, as `GRV` and `RATE` are; a list never glides. **The sound:** the engine keeps what's heard for each slot, the track's own bypass or a lock over it, and hands it to the slot each control block, where the slot keeps running underneath, as before. The fade had been a 10 ms one-pole, about 30 ms to settle, which a lock on one sixteenth would smear across most of it, so it's now a straight crossfade over `BYPASS_FADE_MS`, 5 ms, for `b` and locks alike: long enough not to click, measured in `tests/fx.rs` as no step between frames over a hundredth of the levels' difference and nothing outside the two levels, and short enough to land with its step. A straight crossfade suits it, as what's crossfaded is the same sound before and after the slot. **The FX page:** a bypassed slot was drawn faint; now, while the song plays, it's drawn as it's heard, from the engine's live values, so a slot a lock switches out goes faint and comes back with the lock, as the bars follow what's playing. Stopped, it shows the slot's own, so `b` always shows what it set. Nothing else marks a lock, as the page's visuals stay spartan. A song with no `BYP` plays as it did, and both golden renders are unchanged. Added on 2026-11-09.
89. **A track's `Glide` is how long a `HLD` tie glides its pitch into the next note, in ms, on every voice, and `Off` jumps as before, or on `Acid` slides by its own `Slide`** (#266's second item, Renoise's `-Gxx` done our way). **Prior art:** Renoise's `-Gxx` glides toward a note at a rate in units per tick, so how long it takes depends on the interval and the tempo; the M8 has a pitch slide command with a time; a TB-303's slide is a fixed lag of about 60 ms whatever the interval, and the Devil Fish made it longer; mono synths' portamento, Elektron's included, is a time. **The setting:** `Glide`, a registry setting of the track's, `glide` in files and `GLD` on a step, listed under Machine; the label was free. On `00` to `FF`, `00` is off and `01` to `FF` run from 2 ms to 2 s evenly in ratio, so `80` is 63 ms, about a 303's slide. **Ms rather than a note division:** a glide is how a note is played, as an envelope's times are, which are in ms too (decision 82), and a portamento or a slide sounds the same at any tempo. A division would make a short glide, the usual kind, change with the tempo and `Speed`, and would need very short divisions. Landing on a row is already what `PTCH`'s glide mark does, by rows. The decision against Elektron's speed and multiplier for rates doesn't apply, as this isn't a rate. **The curve:** the same lag as `Acid`'s slide, its time constant a third of its time, in pitch, so it's halfway in pitch at about a quarter of its time and 95% of the way at its time, whatever the interval, as a 303's and an analog portamento's are. A straight line in pitch was considered, which lands exactly, but sounds mechanical next to `Acid`'s. **Which voices:** every one, as the glide is in the voice that plays them: a synth by its pitch, as every synth takes its frequency from the voice, drums and noises included, and a sample by its speed, as a sample's pitch is its speed, so it moves as tape does. **Acid:** off, it slides by its own `Slide`, 60 to 360 ms, as before; set, `Glide` sets the slide's time instead, through its own lag, so neither envelope starts again and its accent carries on as a 303's does. That keeps one setting for every voice's ties, with the 303's knob still its default, and the page shows `Slide` there while `Glide` is off. **When it's read:** as the tie comes, so a `GLD` on the row tied from or the row tied into sets it, as both are the same note (decision 79), and a note that isn't tied never glides. An `ARP`'s notes with `Retrig` off are ties (decision 86), so they glide too. **The track page:** it's under `Speed` in the Sound page's right-hand column, shown as `Off`, dimmed, or a time; `=` and `-` move it one step, `+` and `_` sixteen, `i` takes a time in ms or `off`, and `x` turns it off. It's left out of LFOs' and envelopes' `Dest`, as it's read only as a tie comes. **Left out:** a glide for notes played from the keyboard, which still take `Acid`'s `Slide` and jump elsewhere, as a preview voice doesn't belong to the track's held values; a glide for notes that aren't tied, Elektron's portamento on every note, which `HLD` already marks where it's wanted. At `00` nothing changes, so both golden renders are unchanged sample for sample. Added on 2026-11-09.
90. **Tempo locks are left for later, as a section's tempo rather than a master track's lock** (#266's third item, which the issue left open). Renoise's `ZTxx` sets the tempo from a line, and a lock on the master track's steps would give accelerando and ritardando with its glide mark. Against it, as things are: the tempo is the song's alone, and renders work out their length and every step's frame from one tempo; live recording carries the playhead on from the engine's report at the song's tempo; the engine works steps out ahead of their place (`Published::lead`) and LFOs and delays sync to one tempo; and a glide of tempo needs a tempo that changes within a step, which nothing in the timing has. Each could be done, but together they're a change to the time model rather than a small item, and a half-done one would play differently from its render. The music it's for, a song at about Maschine's scale, mostly wants a section at another tempo, which sections can carry with one tempo each, as Elektron's patterns have their own. So #266 stays open for it, and this is to be revisited with sections. Added on 2026-11-09.
91. **A phrase's order is a walk the track keeps taking, and a pass is the phrase's length in steps whatever the order, so chains and sections keep their lengths; `Pendulum` and `Ping-pong` become `Up-down` and `Up-down-inc`, with `Down-up` and `Down-up-inc` beside them, and the arp gets `Up-down-inc` too** (part of #265, after it closed, as the author's rework of decision 87). **Why:** under decision 87 a pass was one time round the order, so a pendulum of 16 rows made its phrase 30 steps, and its chain and any section it set grew with it; a phrase's length on screen stopped being how long it plays. The author's model is a walk: the order is where the track goes next, and the phrase's length stays its length. Over six steps of a phrase of four, forward plays `1 2 3 4 | 1 2`, up-down `1 2 3 4 | 3 2`, up-down-inc `1 2 3 4 | 4 3`, and pedal `1 2 1 3 | 1 4`. **Prior art:** the Metropolix's tracks each run on with their own lengths and orders, so a pendulum on one drifts against the others; its ORDER has `Pendulum` and `Ping Pong` each with a Forward and Reverse Direction (manual v1.6.5, pp. 70–71), which are these four orders; and arpeggiators name the same shapes up-down and up-down inclusive. **The walk:** a round of an order is the visits it makes before it repeats, one of each row for every order but the four up-and-down ones and `Pedal`, whose rounds are 2n−2 or 2n visits. A slot's phrase walks its round on from pass to pass, so on the chain's lap k a step t into the phrase is k × its length + t along the walk, and the visit there is found by going round the round's pulses. Rounds that visit each row once line up with passes, so `Reverse`, `Odd-even`, `Converge` and `Diverge` play the same each pass, as before; the longer rounds drift over the passes, like a polymeter against the other tracks. As it's worked out from the count, it starts again where the chain does: the top, a section that brings the chain in or resets, a launch or `:reset`, which is where conditions' passes and accumulators start again too. **Pulses:** a row's pulses stay together, so a row whose pulses run past the end of a pass plays the rest of them at the start of the next pass of its phrase. The issue's other choice, finishing the row and shortening the next pass, is the same in time for a phrase alone in its chain, which is what this gives; the walk's form keeps every pass exactly the phrase's length, so chains and sections never vary, and is what the count can work out. In a chain of several phrases the rest of the row waits until its phrase comes round, which plays nothing new on a `SGL` or `HLD`. **Dealt orders:** a `Shuffle`'s round is a pass, and it deals a new one each pass, as decision 87 has it. `Random` still fills each pass with picks, dealt from the pass, but now picks among the rows that fit what the pass has left, so a pick's pulses stay together, and only when none fits, which needs rows of different lengths, is its last pick cut short. Carrying a pick over into the next pass, as fixed orders do, would make each pass depend on the one before, which can't be worked out from the count. **Conditions** count passes of the phrase's length, so `1:2` means every other pass whatever the order, and a row a pass plays twice plays both times or neither; **accumulators** add each time a row plays. **Playing from a row** starts at that row's step as it's written, its forward place, with the walk wherever it is at that step, worked out from the track's count as conditions are, so playing from a row hears what playing up to it would; in reverse, the top row's step plays the bottom row. Starting where the walk first plays the row, as decision 87 did, was the other choice: it plays the cursor's row first, but some rows aren't in the first pass at all under the walk (a pedal of four doesn't play its fourth row until its second pass), and it starts at a step other than the cursor's. **Live changes** keep the row about to play: if the new walk plays it at the same place, nothing moves, which is every edit that leaves the order and the rows' pulses alone; if not, the track moves to where this pass of the new walk plays it, at the same pulse, the nearest if it plays it twice; and if this pass doesn't play it, the track stays where it is and the new walk plays on from there. Restarting the walk was the other choice, but it would jump the track to its chain's start and out of line with the other tracks. **The playhead** and the other tracks' columns come from the same lookups, so they show the walk. **Names:** `up-down` (`1 2 3 4 3 2 1 2`, the ends once), `up-down-inc` (`1 2 3 4 4 3 2 1 1 2`, the ends twice), `down-up` (`4 3 2 1 2 3 4 3`, from the last row) and `down-up-inc` (`4 3 2 1 1 2 3 4 4`), after the reverse Pendulum and Ping Pong, and the list's meanings show two passes of a phrase of four. The arp's modes add `Up-down-inc` after `Up-down`, so both lists use the same words; its `Random` and both golden renders are unchanged. Old names don't load, as there's no backwards compatibility. **Unchanged:** forward walks exactly its rows each pass, so a song without an order plays sample for sample as before. **Alternatives:** decision 87's pass as a time round the order, above; a pendulum alternating direction each pass, which can't play the end rows' turns; and an order per chain slot, still left for later. Added on 2026-11-09.
92. **`Space A` realigns every track by hand while playing: at the next line of the song's launch grid, every track goes back to its chain's start as a `:reset` sends it, once, with the beat carrying on** (#43, the first of M19's sequencer logic). **Prior art:** the Polyend forum's "Meshuggah-style" forced reset, which the issue quotes, wants the snap back played by hand rather than kept on a schedule. Elektron's pattern changes wait for the pattern's end, and the newer machines add `Direct start`, which changes at once from the top; Ableton's clip launches wait for the global quantisation, a bar unless changed, from `None` to 8 bars; the Octatrack waits for the pattern's end as well. Each lets a press land on the music's grid rather than where the finger did. **The key:** `Space A`, beside `Space m`, `Space s` and fill's `Space l`, where the live controls are, in every view that has them: the song, chain and phrase views, the track page, the mixer, and the audio and project pages, so `Space k` lists it in each. It's a capital as it's the wider of a pair, every track, after the author's rule that a capital is the wider version of its lowercase key, as `Space M` clears every mute; `Space a` is left free for realigning the cursor's track alone, should per-track resets (#44) want a key. **When it lands:** on the next line of the launch grid, `Launch`, which `:launch` and the project page set, rather than a setting of its own, so one setting says when a played change lands, as Ableton's global quantisation does for every launch. The lines are counted as a launch's are, from the start of the section's current repeat, so a section shorter than the grid has a line only at its start. A bar unless changed, so a press that's a little late waits for the next bar rather than being heard as a mistake, which the issue worried the next step would be; `:launch step` gives the next step, and `:launch 1/4` the next beat. With `:launch chain`, launches wait for each track's own chain to end, but those ends fall at different places, which is what a realign is for, so it waits for the bar. A press while one is waiting takes it back, as a mistaken press otherwise couldn't be. **What starts again** is what `:reset` and a section that resets start again, as all three go through `Engine::realign`, so a realign is the start of every track's chain: each track's count goes back to 0, so a groove's pairs, and swing's, count from the line, as #43 asked; each chain plays from its top, a launched one included, with its lanes in line again; conditions count passes from the first again, so `1ST` plays again (decision 84); accumulators set to `Reset` `Chain` or to a number of loops go back to the notes as written, the loops counted from the line, while those set to `Play` carry on (decision 85); each phrase's walk starts again from its first row (decision 91); and each track at a speed of its own lines up with the song's step there, a longer step cut short, as at a section's start (decision 82). A section's `:reset` counts its steps from the realign, as it counts them from a resetting section's start. What isn't a chain's start carries on: the section's own place, so it ends and moves on when it would have and the launch grid stays where it was; the beat; LFOs, on the song's time as at a `:reset`; what `PRE` and `NEI` read, which only playing forgets; and the random streams. **Not undone:** it moves where playback is rather than the song, so it's no undo step and doesn't mark the song changed. It's a live control in the engine, `Command::Realign`, which the engine resolves against its own state, as `Tab` is, never saved and never in a render. It works only while playing, as playing starts every track at its start anyway, and stopping, pausing or playing from somewhere forgets one waiting. **Shown:** the status line says `Every track realigns on the 1bar grid; Space A again takes it back`, or `on the next bar` with launches at a chain's end, then `Realign taken back` on a second press, and `Realign works while playing` when stopped; while it waits, the transport says `REALIGN` after `playing`, as it says `FILL`, from a flag in the engine's report, so it goes when the realign lands. **Per-track resets** (#44) aren't built, and this doesn't stand in their way: it uses the one place all tracks share, which they would split, and leaves `Space a` for a track of its own. **Alternatives:** the next step, the issue's simplest, which `:launch step` still gives; a quantise of its own for realigning, which would be a second setting for when played changes land; Bitwig's launch tolerance, which takes a press just after a line as on it and jumps in late, left for later as it would cut a step already heard; and `Space a` for every track, which would leave nothing wider for the capital. Added on 2026-11-09.
93. **Kitty images can be as big as the screen: placeholders number all 297 rows and columns, a picture can have a few colours that each mean something, and a big one is compressed inside its PNG** (#273, for the sample page's waveform, #274). **Placeholders:** `kitty::POSITIONS` now has all 297 marks of kitty's `rowcolumn-diacritics.txt`, which Ghostty's `graphics_unicode.zig` lists the same, in the same order, so an image covers up to `MOST_CELLS`, 297 characters, across and down. The 28 it had were the start of that list, not the protocol's limit, as decision 75 read them. Placeholders were kept over direct placement, the issue's other choice, as they're text: windows draw over them, ratatui moves them, and the cell backgrounds under them can be drawn, as decisions 56 and 75 have it. **Colours:** `kitty::Picture` is a drawing whose pixels are `0` for nothing, which lets the cell's background show, or the number of one of up to 15 colours, which are for things that need telling apart, such as slices, the loop, marks and pending renders, and never for decoration. Its PNG takes 1, 2 or 4 bits a pixel as the colours need, with the first palette entry see-through as before. The cursor, the selection and the playhead aren't in it: they're cell backgrounds behind it, so they move without sending the image again. The small drawings are pictures of one colour, and their PNGs are byte for byte what they were, which a test pins by size and CRC for the Sound page's waveform, the explorer's preview, the kit picker's and an envelope. **Sending:** `examples/big_image.rs` draws the sample page's waveform, 160 by 14 characters at the author's 15 by 39 pixels each, so 2400 by 546 pixels in four colours, and times ten sends each way in kitty 0.50, from starting to write to the terminal's `OK`, so the terminal's decoding counts. The median: a stored PNG, as the small drawings are sent, was 876,556 bytes and 13.2 ms; with `o=z` at zlib's level 1, 47,923 bytes and 12.2 ms, and at level 6, 15,827 bytes and 11.9 ms; a PNG compressing its own data at level 6, 15,518 bytes, 2.2 ms to make and 10.8 ms; raw RGBA with `o=z`, 270,400 bytes and 20.8 ms; and through shared memory (`t=s`), a stored PNG in 6.2 ms and raw RGBA in 4.9 ms. `docs/terminal-notes.md` has the table. **The choice:** a picture whose pixels fit in one stored zlib block, 64 KB, as every small drawing's do, is stored as before, and a bigger one is compressed inside its PNG with `flate2` (miniz_oxide) at zlib's default level. That's 56 times fewer bytes than stored, so writing it never waits on the terminal to read it, and it's as small as `o=z` and a little quicker, with no key that kitty and Ghostty treat differently: kitty refuses a compressed PNG without `S=`, its size before compressing, and Ghostty doesn't need it. The PNG is one any reader takes, so `just screenshots` puts it in as it is. `o=z` was the issue's suggestion and works with `S=`; compressing raw pixels was slower than either. Shared memory halves the round trip, but needs `shm_open` and `mmap` through `unsafe` code, and an object the terminal never reads stays until the machine restarts; at about 11 ms a send, made only on zoom, scroll or edit, compressing is enough, and shared memory is left for a profile that shows sends matter. **Fewer pixels to a dot:** the small drawings use blocks of 3 by 3 pixels for a dot, so kitty's scaling keeps their edges crisp. A big picture should instead be drawn one pixel to each of the screen's, at the terminal's own character size from `crossterm::terminal::window_size`, as kitty fits an image to its characters keeping its aspect ratio; that's #274's to do, and the example draws what it shows that way. **Cell backgrounds** under see-through pixels: kitty's docs say the background shows, and the example draws a selection, a cursor and a playhead moving every frame under the image to see it. It wasn't seen by eye here, as this session couldn't take a screenshot, and Ghostty isn't installed, so both are on `docs/terminal-notes.md`'s list to check by hand. Added on 2026-11-09.
94. **A project's samples are a bank, numbered `01` to `FF` with `00` as none, and a sampler track's `Sample` setting, `SMP` on a step, picks the one each note plays, so a lock plays another sample for its note as an Elektron's sample lock does; each entry keeps how it plays, its gain and tune for now, and the machine is now `Sampler`** (#189, the first step of the sample editor's design, #268, whose comment on #189 settled the per-entry settings, `BankEntry`, content hashes and `SMP`). **Prior art:** a Digitakt keeps a project's samples in numbered slots, and a track's sample is a parameter whose lowest value is `OFF`, so a parameter lock on it is a sample lock and an LFO can move it; an Octatrack's slots keep their own settings; a tracker's instrument column chooses what a step plays. **The bank:** `Song.bank`, a map from number to `BankEntry { label, name, edit: SampleEdit }`, saved in `song.ron`. A `SampleEdit` is one `Timeline` segment over one whole file, its `Source` a path and a 128-bit XXH3 hash of the file's bytes (the `xxhash-rust` crate, BSL-1.0), and `PlayParams { gain_db, tune }`; the points, slices, marks, recipe and hints of #268's model arrive with #269 onward, and need nothing here to change. **Size:** 255 entries, `01` to `FF`, as `SMP`'s value is a byte, two hex digits on a step. `00` is none rather than a 256th entry, as the Digitakt has it: a new track is silent rather than quietly playing the first sample, and a step can lock `SMP 00` to silence one hit. 255 is twice a Digitakt II's 127 project slots and far past a song at about Maschine's scale; more would need a wider value than a step has. **Numbers stay put:** removing an entry leaves a gap, which the next file fills, as an Elektron's slots do, so a lock never needs rewriting. Slices will renumber (#270), and those locks are `SLI`'s, not `SMP`'s. **The setting:** in the registry as the sampler's own, a new owner, `Owner::Sampler`, which `SLI`, the start, `SLEN` and `PLY` will join; stored as `sampler.sample`, labelled `SMP`, which was free. It's a list, `Off` then `01` to `FF`, so it has no fine part and never glides, and it exists only on a track whose machine is the sampler. So locks, holds, lanes, `K`, typing the label and the step text come from the registry, as for `1BYP`. **Modulation:** unlike `GRV` and `RATE`, LFOs and envelopes may move it, as a Digitakt's LFO can move its sample slot: a whole entry for each hex step of depth, read as each note starts, so the bank can be played through by an LFO. **When it's read:** as a note starts, from the held value or a lock over it, with what modulation adds, and the voice keeps that sample until it ends, as before. A lock ends with the next note, `h` holds it, and a number with nothing in it plays nothing. **The engine** never sees the bank: `Published::new` resolves each number on the edit thread to a `Playable`, the pool's sample with its gain as a factor and its tune as a speed, in a fixed table of 256 the callback indexes, as it resolved each track's path before. It's the start of #268's `Playable`, which #269 grows into spans. **Per entry, not per track:** a kit's gain and tune were the track's `Level` and `Pitch`; now they're the entry's, so a kit's sound plays the same on any track, and the track's `Level` and `Pitch` come on top. A kit's label names the entry and still names the track, and its choke group stays the track's, as choking is between tracks. **A file joins once:** adding a file finds an entry that plays it, by path or by content, and the pool loads each file once however many entries and tracks play it. A kit's sound is the same entry only if it plays the same file the same way, as a kit can tune one file twice for two toms; a file chosen in the explorer takes the entry already there, as the song's copy of an entry wins (#268). **Hashes at work:** opening a project checks each source against its hash: a file that's gone is looked for by content in the project's `samples/` folder and the bank pointed at it, and one that's changed since it joined the bank is reported. `:w path` copies each content into `samples/` once. Plain `:w` still refers to files where they are. **The page:** the Sound page's `File` row is now `Sample`, showing `03 BD Kick`; `=` and `-` step through the entries the bank has, `x` sets `Off`, and `K` lists the bank with each entry's waveform beside it, as the explorer's preview draws it, and `Add a file` at the end, which opens the explorer, as `Space 5` still does. `K` on an `SMP`'s value lists the bank too, and choosing `SMP` for an empty slot lists it at once, as `CND` lists its conditions. The status line names the entry and its file. `:bank` lists the bank for the track. **Removing:** `:bank clean` removes, as one undo step, the entries no track's `Sample` and no step's `SMP` names; one only an LFO or an envelope reaches isn't counted. Nothing is removed by itself, as an Elektron never unloads a slot unasked. **The format:** `FORMAT_VERSION` is 6, `Track.sample` is gone, and there's no migration, so songs saved before, such as the test projects in `~/Music/vimtracker/projects/`, need saving again from a matching version or making again. **Left out:** slices and marks (#270, #271), per-file defaults (#278), and finding a moved file anywhere but the project's `samples/`. Both golden renders are unchanged, as a gain of 0 dB and a tune of 0 multiply by exactly 1. Added on 2026-11-09.
95. **A track's `Reset` starts its chain again every so many of the song's steps, counted from where its chain last started, and `Space a` does it once by hand for the cursor's track, on the launch grid** (#44, M19's sequencer logic). **Prior art:** Elektron's master length, which sends every track back to its start after up to 1024 steps while each track loops at its own length, here per track, as the Polyend forum post the issue quotes asks, so a 5-step part reset every 16 makes one fixed, syncopated bar while a 7-step part drifts against it. **The issue's worry** that the engine keeps one place for all tracks is out of date: since `Speed` (decision 82) each track has its own clock, count and chain start, and the phrase view's other-track columns, its playheads and recording all work from those, so a track's reset needs no new place, only its chain started again. **The setting:** `Reset` on the Sound page's right-hand column under `Speed`, as both are about the track's time, `Track.reset` as a number of steps from 1 to 1024, the most Elektron's master length has, `reset` in files and left out when off. It shows `Off`, whole bars as `:launch` names them, such as `1bar`, and anything else in steps, such as `5-step`; the status line says `its chain starts again every 5 steps of the song`. `i` takes steps, in hex after `0x`, bars such as `2bar`, or `off`; `=` and `-` move it a step, down from 1 to off; `+` and `_` move it to the next whole bar, below a bar to off; and `x` turns it off. It isn't a step's lock or in `K`'s list: it's a schedule for the track rather than a value that changes from step to step, as a section's `:reset` isn't. **What it counts:** the song's sixteenths, whatever the track's speed, so `1bar` is a bar at `1/8` too, as `:reset` and launches count sixteenths (decision 82). It counts from where the track's chain last started: the top, a section bringing in a new chain, a launch, a realign, or its own last reset, so it's always that many steps of the chain, and a launched part keeps its bar from its launch. That's where the track's clock last lined up, which the engine already keeps, so the check is a subtraction. A track playing from a row of its chain counts it from that chain's top. On a reset that falls partway through a slower track's step, the step is cut short, as at a section's start. **A chain start:** a reset starts the chain as a section bringing it in does: from its top with its lanes in line, conditions counting passes from the first (decision 84), accumulators set to `Chain` or to loops starting again (decision 85), and the phrase's walk starting again (decision 91). **The groove carries on**, unlike `:reset` and `Space A`, which start every track's count again together: a track's groove counts its steps from the song's start or the last of those (decision 80), so a track's reset doesn't move which of the song's sixteenths it swings, however odd the reset. With the bar a reset usually is, the two are the same. **With a section's `:reset`:** both apply. The section's realigns every track, and a track's reset counts from where its chain last started, so it counts again from the section's: with `Reset` at 8 and `:reset 12`, the track starts again at 8, 12, 20, 24 and so on. Where they fall together, the section's realigns everything and the track starts once, so neither wins over the other. A section that starts with `reset` is the same. **Playing from a step** hears what playing up to it would: working out the start walks each track's resets as it walks the sections. Auditioning a row while stopped takes the track's reset into account. **`Space a`:** the cursor's track's chain starts again at the next line of the launch grid, once, as `Space A` does for every track (decision 92), and a second press takes it back. It's the lowercase of the pair, as `Space m` is to `Space M`. Like a reset, it's a chain start that keeps the groove counting, so the track comes back in line with the song's swing; `Space A` landing on the same line takes it with it. It works only while playing, says `Realign works while playing` when stopped and `Return A plays no chain to realign` on a fixed track, and stopping, pausing or playing from somewhere forgets it. It's not an undo step. The status line says `Track 01 realigns on the 1bar grid; Space a again takes it back`, then `Track 01's realign taken back`, and the transport shows `REALIGN` while any realign waits, from the engine's report. **Left out:** a per-section track reset, which a section's chain choice already covers by bringing a chain in; drawing where the next reset falls on the phrase view, which the playhead going back to the top already shows; and resets counted in the track's own steps, which would make `1bar` mean a different length at each speed. Added on 2026-11-09.
96. **A `List` fill goes round up to eight values typed into one `Values` row, in place of `From` and `To`, counting only the rows it changes, with `Run` giving each value that many rows and `.` keeping the list** (#97, M19's sequencer logic, built to the issue's proposed design). **Prior art:** Polyend's Fill tool, which `Where` already follows, has only `Constant`, `From-To` and `Random` (tracker-ce's `gPatternEdit.cpp`), and an Elektron's velocity locks are set step by step, so an accent of three values is typed by hand on both. A short list of values going round under the notes is what the Metropolix's mod lanes give with a length of their own (the M12 lanes design), live; this writes it once as plain values, as the panel does everything. Counting only the rows changed is `Alternate`'s rule, so a three-value accent stays in order however the hits are spaced, rather than falling on every third row. **The data:** `Shape.values`, a `Values` of a fixed `[u8; 8]` and a length, so a shape stays plain data that's `Copy`; eight is the issue's cap, and fits the panel's row as `70 40 55 ...` in 23 characters. **The panel:** `List` comes after `Alternate` in `Fill`'s turn and list, described `A few values in turn`. Its rows are `Fill`, `Values`, `Run` and `Where`, as `Curve` and `S` hide for a wave. `Values` is typing only, as the issue has it: `i` starts empty, as on every other row, two hex digits make a value and the space after it comes by itself, so `704055` types `70 40 55`, and a space ends a value of one digit sooner, as `7 ` is `07`. A ninth value isn't taken, `Backspace` takes back one character, `Enter` keeps it, `Esc` leaves the list as it was, and `Enter` on nothing is refused with the status line saying `Values are hex values like 70 40 55, up to 8 of them`. A value over the column's top is kept at the top, as typing `From` does. `=` and `-` on it say `Type the values with i`. **Choices the issue left open:** the list starts as `From` and `To`, the two ends the panel opened with or was set to, so switching from `Alternate` to `List` sounds the same until the list is typed, and `x` on `Values` puts that back; a list once typed stays while `Fill` moves through the others and back. `~`, which swaps the ends, turns the list round, as swapping the ends of an `Alternate` does, and the hint under the rows says `~ reverse the list`. `i` replacing the whole list, rather than starting with it to edit, was chosen to match the panel's other rows; with eight values at most it's a short retype. **Left for later,** as the issue has it: merging `Alternate` into `List`, and nudging one value with `=` and `-` by selecting within the row. Added on 2026-11-09.
97. **A sampler plays a span of its bank entry, compiled on the edit thread into a `Playable`: the entry's start, end and loop, in frames at its file's own rate, its gain, tune and cents, reverse and fades, cut by the track's `Start` (`STA`), `Length` (`SLEN`) and `Play` (`PLY`: one-shot, loop or ping-pong), with a declick wherever it cuts into the sound, and a point moved under a playing note heard at once** (#269, the second step of the sample editor's design, #268). **Prior art:** the Digitakt's `STRT`, `LEN`, `LOOP` and loop position, track parameters a step can lock and an LFO can move, with the length counted on its own rather than from the start; the Octatrack's trim and loop points in samples, kept per slot; the Polyend Tracker's start, end and two loop points with one-shot, forward, backward and ping-pong loops; the Deluge's markers in frames at the file's own rate and its loop crossfade into the margin before the loop's start, linear as its time stretcher's are, "less jarring" on short hops (`model/voice/voice_sample.cpp`). **The model:** `SampleEdit` gains `points: Points { start, end: Option, loop: Option<Loop { start, end, crossfade }> }`, all in the file's own frames, and `PlayParams` gains `cents`, `reverse`, `fade_in` and `fade_out`, the fades in the file's frames too. Every new field has a default and is left out of `song.ron` when it has it, so older songs open as they were and `FORMAT_VERSION` stays 6. **Whose is the mode:** the entry has the loop's place and the track has whether it loops, as a Digitakt's sample has its loop position and its track `LOOP`: the track's settings act on whichever entry plays (decision 94), so a lock can play one hit of a pad looped and the next once. An entry with no loop of its own loops all that plays. **`Start` and `Length`:** shares of the entry's span, `00` to `FF`, as the Digitakt's are, with `FF` all of it: the note starts `Start` of the way in and plays `Length` of the whole from there, stopping at the span's end. They count in the order it plays, so on a reversed entry they count back from its end. They're continuous, with a fine part, a step locks them and an LFO or an envelope moves them, read as each note starts, as `SMP` is. `PLY` is a list, `One-shot`, `Loop` and `Ping-pong`, which modulation can't move, as with `GRV` and `RATE`, since a way of playing is chosen rather than swept. The labels are `STA`, `SLEN` (the author's, as `LEN` is `Gate`'s, decision 78) and `PLY`, stored as `sampler.start`, `sampler.length` and `sampler.play`. Thru comes with slices (#270). **The `Playable`:** in `vt_model::playable`, so the engine never reads a `SampleEdit`: `Playable::of(entry, pool)` resolves the file, turns points into the pool's frames, clamps them to the file and the crossfade to the room before the loop and half its length, and gives an `Arc` of the frames and a table of spans, one for now, each a `Copy` `Span` with its start, end, loop, reverse, fades, gain and speed. `Published.bank` is an `Arc<[Option<Arc<Playable>>]>`; `Published::after` keeps the last version's whole table when the bank and pool are the same, and otherwise each entry's `Arc` when the entry is equal and its file the same `Arc`, so only a changed entry is worked out again, and the app keeps the version it last sent for this. **The file's rate:** the pool now keeps the rate each file was recorded at beside the rate it was converted to (`SamplePool::load`), so a point at a 44.1 kHz file's frame 4410 plays from the program's frame 4800 at 48 kHz. **The voice** reads in play order, frames counted from the file's first or, reversed, its last, so reversing is only how a place becomes a frame, and loops, fades and ends work the same either way. A start or an end that cuts into the sound fades over a declick, `DECLICK_FRAMES`, at least; at the file's own edges there's none, so a whole file plays bit for bit as before and both golden renders are unchanged. The fade in runs from the note's start, and the fade out to the end only for a one-shot, as a looping note never reaches its end. **Loops:** a forward loop's crossfade runs from before the loop's start, the Deluge's pre-margin, so it's over as the loop starts again. It's a linear crossfade, equal in gain, as a loop's two ends are usually alike and an equal-power one swells a steady sound by 3 dB. With none set it's a declick long, and with no room before the start, a loop from the file's first frame, it runs on past the end instead. The mix is made live by a second read head, only while it lasts, rather than baked into a tail when compiling: the loop `Start` and `Length` make differs from note to note, and the same head smooths a loop moved under a playing note. Ping-pong turns at either end, which is smooth already, so it has no crossfade. **Heard at once:** when a version arrives, each voice and waiting note playing an entry whose file is the same takes its new span from the frame it's on: one past its new end fades out over a declick, one past its loop goes round with a crossfade, a reversed one reads on from the same frame the other way, a new gain glides over about a millisecond, and a new tune moves its pitch. **Setting points:** until the sample page (#274), `:sample` takes a word: `start`, `end`, `loop from to [crossfade]` or `loop off`, `reverse` (`on`, `off` or neither to turn it over), `fade in out`, `gain dB` and `tune semitones [cents]`, with times as `1.5` seconds or `250ms`. Each changes the entry the current track's `Sample` is on, as one undo step, and the status line says what it is now. They're words of `:sample`, as #268 names `:sample default`, since `:loop` keeps the section. **The page:** `Start`, `Length` and `Play` sit under `Sample` on the Sound page, where a synth's settings are, so a sampler's page has a synth's shape: bars for the two shares, and the mode's name, which `K` lists. **Left out:** Thru, slices and per-slice settings (#270), snapping to zero crossings and normalising (#272), drawing the points on the Sound page's waveform, which the sample page does (#274), loop to tempo (#286), and a row for cents, which only `:sample tune` sets. Added on 2026-11-09.
98. **Each sample file has an analysis, kept by its content hash in the cache folder: a min/max peak pyramid, its peak level and where its sound is. `:normalize` is a gain worked out from it and `:trim` sets the entry's start and end from it, so neither writes audio, and `u` takes either back** (#272, the fourth step of the sample editor's design, #268). **Prior art:** Ableton's `.asd` keeps a sample's analysis beside it, which #268 rules out for us, as we never write next to the user's files; Audacity and REAPER keep peak summaries at several resolutions, Audacity's finest every 256 samples; Bitwig's Normalize writes a gain on the event rather than audio; tracker-ce's normaliser scales each channel by its own peak, which shifts a stereo sample's balance. **Where it's kept:** `vimtracker/analysis/<hash>`, the hash as 32 hex digits, in `$XDG_CACHE_HOME` if it's set, on any system, as the config folder follows `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, or else in `~/Library/Caches` on macOS and `~/.cache` elsewhere. Never in the song, never in the project, never beside a sample. **The file** is a small binary format of our own, since RON would be megabytes of text for a long file: `VTAN`, `ANALYSIS_VERSION`, the hash, rate, channels, length, peak, the sound's range, then each level's lowest and highest samples as little-endian `f32`s. One of another version, of another content, or cut short is built again and written over. It's written to a `.part` file and renamed, so a reader never sees half of one. **What's in it,** at the file's own rate and in its own channels, as an entry's points are: the pyramid, whose finest buckets are 256 frames, as Audacity's are, up to one bucket over the whole file, which is 1.8 MB for 5 minutes of stereo at 48 kHz (each halving of the finest bucket would double it, and a view closer than 256 frames a column reads the frames themselves, the sample page's business in #274); the peak, of whichever channel is louder; and the sound, from the first frame above -60 dBFS in any channel to just past the last. Onsets, the Deluge's perc measure and a tempo guess join it with #277 and #285, raising the version. **Reading it:** `fold` and `peak` over any stretch read at most two buckets a level, so O(log n), exact on the finest buckets' edges and otherwise covering the buckets either end is in; `view` reads each column from the level whose buckets are nearest its width without being wider, so O(columns) at any zoom. **Where it's built:** in the program, on a thread of its own, `Analyst`, which takes every file asked for in turn, unlike the explorer's loader, which reads only the newest. Each time the app publishes a version, it asks for every bank file with no analysis yet, so a file joining the bank, a project opening and an undo all ask without a call of their own. Analyses live in the `SamplePool`, by hash, so `Playable::of` finds a file's peak without a new argument, and `Published::after` works an entry out again when its peak has come in since the last version, rather than keeping the last version's `Playable`, and a device switch keeps them, as they don't depend on the rate. A hit in the cache is trusted by its hash. A miss reads and hashes the file, and keeps the analysis under the hash of what it read, so a file changed since it joined the bank never gets another content's analysis; that entry plays as it is, and opening the project has already said it changed. `vimtracker render`, `bench`, `:render` and `:render --stems` wait for every analysis, so a render never depends on how far work in the background had got. **Normalise:** `PlayParams.normalize`, a peak level in dBFS from -60 to 0. `:normalize -0.3` sets it, `:normalize` alone brings the peak to 0 dBFS, and `:normalize off` clears it. The gain is the level over the file's peak, the same for every channel, so a stereo sample keeps its balance, and the entry's own gain, a kit's for instance, comes on top, as the track's `Level` comes on top of both. It's worked out on the edit thread into the gain of the `Playable`'s span, in `Playable::of`, read as each note starts as before, so it never clicks. Until the analysis is in, and for a silent file, it's 1. **`:trim`:** sets the entry's start and end to the sound's, with no end when the sound runs to the file's end, waiting for the analysis if it's on its way. -60 dBFS keeps a one-shot's tail without its noise floor; tracker-ce's export trims at about -72 dBFS. **Points:** `:trim` sets the `start` and `end` of the `SampleEdit.points` that #269 brought (decision 97), in frames at the file's own rate, and leaves its loop where it was. The sampler plays them, cut further by the track's `STA` and `SLEN`, so a trimmed entry is heard only from where its sound starts to where it ends. **Neither writes a file:** both are one undo step through `SetBankEntry`, as `:bank clean` is, and the source is never touched. Neither changes the format version, as both settings are left out of `song.ron` until they're set. **Left out:** a size limit and eviction for the cache folder, as analyses are small and the render cache's limits (#281) can cover both; a level for `:trim`, as in `:trim -48`, which would need the frames rather than the cached scan; normalising a slice, from `peak` over its range, with #270; and showing the level on the Sound page, which waits for the sample page. **Measured** on an Apple M5, release build, 5 minutes of stereo at 48 kHz (`cargo run --release -p vt-model --example analysis_bench`): building the pyramid 55 to 80 ms; the whole analysis from a 16-bit WAV, with reading, hashing and decoding it, 165 to 215 ms; the cache file 1.8 MB, read back in under a millisecond; a view 2400 columns wide 4 to 6 µs at every zoom from the whole file to 73 ms, and 160 columns wide under 0.5 µs; the peak of nearly the whole file 30 ns. Added on 2026-11-09.
99. **In the sample explorer a kit opens like a folder to its sounds, a sound with variants or layers opens to its files, and the kit folders and Ableton Live's sample library are places** (#303, M22). **Prior art:** Ableton's browser unfolds a Live Set or a rack in place with its arrow, while `Enter` or a double-click loads the thing itself, and its Drum Racks are what the imported kits came from; Elektron's +Drive and the M8's browser are folders of files, which the explorer already follows. **The kit:** any `.ron` that `load_kit` reads is listed as a folder, named by the kit, such as `909 Core Kit`, after the real folders as a file is, with the kit's preview beside it as `Space 8` shows it; a project's `song.ron` doesn't read as a kit, so it isn't listed. **Its sounds** are the rows the kit in use lists, label first with the name beside it, and choosing one is choosing it from that group: the bank entry takes its label, name, gain and tune, and the track its label and choke group. A sound with no label now shows its name rather than a blank, in that group too. **Variants and layers,** after Ableton's arrow: a sound with either has an arrow, `Enter` chooses the sound itself, its main file, and `l` opens it to one plain list of its own file, `Main`, its variants, `Variant 1` on, and its velocity layers, `Layer 1 soft` to the hardest, such as `Layer 4 hard`, kept short so a long file's name fits beside it, each by its file's name with that beside it. Any of them is chosen in the sound's place, with the sound's label, name, gain, tune and choke group, so a variant is a different file under the same name; the bank list shows the file beside it. A layer that is the main file again, as Ableton's imports have it, is listed as the kit says. The tree knows a sound by its place under the kit's path, as `909 Core Kit.ron/0`, and a file by its place under the sound's, so `H`, `Backspace` and a search keep them as they keep files. **Audition and preview:** a kit's sound has a sample's preview and plays as the cursor moves onto it, with `A` and `P` as for files, the file as it is rather than with the kit's gain and tune, as files play; this holds for the kit in use's group too. **A missing file** is listed, its preview says why it can't be read, as any unreadable file's does, and choosing it leaves the status line saying so, as `:sample` does. **Places:** recent, the project, `kits` (the settings' kit folders), the settings' `samples`, then `library`, then here and home: the library is the largest, so it comes last of the program's own. `library` is the `Samples` folder, with `Loops`, `One Shots` and `Multisamples`, of the Live that `kits import-ableton` finds by default, the newest in `/Applications`; it's found once as the program starts, kept as `App::library`, and none in tests, rather than a new setting, which would only ever say where Live is. **Left for later:** `L` on a kit in the sample explorer to put it on tracks, as `Space 8` does; auditioning with a kit's gain and tune; layers playing by velocity; and a chosen kit sound noting its kit's folder as a recent place, as its pick doesn't know the kit. Added on 2026-11-09.
100. **The picker scrolls with a margin of three rows beyond the cursor, held on the `Picker` rather than worked out from the cursor, and has Vim's `zz`, `zt`, `zb`, `C-d` and `C-u`** (the author's report of 2026-11-09, no issue). Every `K` list, the explorer, the sample browser and the command line's list of matches shared a view that scrolled only once the cursor was on the last row, so going down a long list never showed what was below it. **Prior art:** Vim's popup menu, the command line's list's model, keeps up to 3 rows of context round the selected match (`pum_set_selected`'s "a few lines of context"); Vim's own `scrolloff` is 5 in `defaults.vim` and 0 in Neovim; snacks.nvim's picker takes the window's `scrolloff`, shrunk to half the list's height less one, which with the author's Neovim setting of 2 gives 2; Telescope has no margin, only `scroll_strategy`, and its `C-d` and `C-u` scroll the preview. **The margin:** 3, Vim's popup menu's number, about a fifth of the 16 rows a list shows and the 18 the explorer shows, and the same in the command line's list of 10. The author keeps Neovim's at 2 because a big `scrolloff` costs `C-e` and `C-y` runway, which the picker doesn't have, so the extra row is only more of what's coming. A list too short for it keeps half its rows less one, as snacks does, and the margin gives way at the list's ends, with no space after the last row. **State:** the first row shown is a field of the `Picker`, which moves only as far as the margin needs, so the view holds as the cursor turns round and after a `zz`; drawing records the rows the list had room for, which `zz` and `C-d` go by, and fits the view to that room, as ratatui's `ListState` keeps its offset. Searching, opening and closing a category or folder, `gg`, `G`, and the command line's Tab, which moves the cursor from outside, all fit it again rather than resetting it. **Keys:** `zz`, `zt` and `zb` put the cursor's row in the middle, at the top or at the bottom, keeping the margin as Vim does with `scrolloff`. Nothing shows before the first row, but `zz` and `zt` show space after the last, as Vim shows the space after a buffer's last line, so the last rows can sit in the middle or at the top; moving keeps that space until the margin needs the view back, and moving alone never makes it (the author found `zt` did nothing in a 22-row `K` list, which could only scroll 6 rows); `z` now waits for another key as `g` does, so a search for a name starting with `z` needs `/`. `C-d` and `C-u` move the cursor and the view half the list's rows, as Vim does, in the search too, as snacks binds them and as `C-n` and `C-p` work there; a count before them is ignored rather than setting the amount as Vim's `scroll` does. **`Esc`:** in the search it goes back to the list, keeping the search and what it found, as `Esc` leaves Insert mode and as snacks' picker leaves its input for its list, so `j`, `k` and `zz` move through the matches; only the list's own `Esc` closes. Before, it cleared the search, and closed a list that had none, so a list opened on its search, as the FX commands and tracks are, closed on the first `Esc` (the author's report of 2026-11-09). A list opened on its search now takes two. **Other lists:** the command line's list is the same picker and view, so it has the margin too. The chain view's slots, the song view and the guides scroll by their own code and weren't changed. Added on 2026-11-09.
